Metroidvania Toolkit — Creator Master Guide#

This is the master guide for making an exploration platformer with the Metroidvania Toolkit. It assumes you have never used Tiled or Defold. Follow the first-world walkthrough in order once; after that, use the recipes and quick reference when you return to your project.

The supported workflow is visual and Tiled-first. You create rooms and place objects in Tiled, arrange rooms in a Tiled world or the Layout Editor, run menu commands in Defold, and play. You should not need to edit Lua code or anything under mvtk/.

Scope: The toolkit supports full-length exploration games with traversal, combat, enemies and bosses, encounters, collectibles, gates, hazards, NPCs and dialogue, audio/visual zones, world state, quests, equipment and progression. Section 10 catalogs the shipped templates; the recipes explain how to use them.
Beginner note: Menu names in this guide are written exactly as they appear. File ▸ new_room_blank means open the File menu in Tiled and click new_room_blank. Text in code style is a filename, folder, property, or value.

Start here — make three rooms, then play#

You only need this path to get started. Use the linked instructions when you need help. The object recipes, art options, and configuration reference later in this guide are for features you choose to add.

Step Do this You are done when
1. Open the tools Extract the toolkit. Open game.project in Defold and run Project ▸ Set Up This Toolkit. In Tiled, tick the defold and lua plugins under Edit ▸ Preferences... ▸ Plugins, open MVTK.tiled-project and click Enable Extensions if Tiled asks. Tiled has File ▸ new_room_blank and File ▸ Export Current Room.
2. Make three rooms Make a tileset for your art, with collision shapes on its solid tiles. Then follow the room walkthrough: use your art's tile size, paint the room, collision and door markers, and place one player start. Save/export each room. All three rooms have a .lua and .tilemap export.
3. Prepare the game Stop the game. In Defold run Reload Editor Scripts → Prepare & Check → Build, in that order. Exact clicks. Validation passes and the title screen opens.
4. Connect and play Open Layout Editor, click Rescan Rooms, click each room in the Rooms list to place it, drag the rooms until matching doors snap, click Export, wait, then Menu → Play Game with an empty slot. On Choose difficulty, select Normal for this first pass. Connect rooms, then play. You can walk between the rooms, save, quit, and continue.

Keep the starting presets for this first pass. Use Game Settings after Prepare & Check when you want to change movement or abilities. Stop the game first, open Project ▸ Game Settings…, then save and Build to test. You do not need to tune every setting before making your first world.

The room layers you need:

Layer Put this on it
bounds An unbroken one-tile ring marking the room footprint. Keep it closed at doors too.
art/environment Visible art. Tiles with Tile Collision Editor shapes block the player on any layer that does not scroll. Use tiles without collision shapes for decoration.
solid (optional) Invisible collision: paint tiles with collision shapes here and hide the layer.
grapple Paint where the hook may catch. Add visible hook markers on the art layers; this layer adds no art or player collision.
ice (optional) Paint over floor cells for slippery ground. Hidden tags bake into editable Ice Floor strips; keep the solid floor underneath and Parallax X / Y at 1.
exit/door_left, door_right, door_top, door_bottom Exit markers where rooms should connect. Leave a real opening in the art/collision.
templates Ready-made objects, including the one intended player_start.

If a step fails, fix its first error before continuing. The troubleshooting section is arranged in the same order. Do not edit generated room files or framework code to get past an error.

What the toolkit can do#

Choose a group below to jump to its recipes. Add systems as your game needs them; each recipe explains the settings and any switch needed to turn it on.

What you want to make Recipes to start with
Reuse objects and bring in your own art Object presets, player art, enemy art
Build a connected world with regions, maps and travel World layout, region titles, fast travel, checkpoints and teleporters, Warp Statues
Unlock movement and build routes through rooms Abilities and gates, High Jump, traversal, water, vehicles and elevators, wind, zip lines
Open shortcuts and change the world with story progress Switches and shortcuts, Strike Switches, switch-order gates, object conditions, terrain conditions
Hide secrets, place hazards and build object puzzles Hazards, secrets and breakable walls, breakable pots, carry and throw, sockets
Tune player attacks, energy costs and healing Game Settings ▸ Attacks, healing, elements, player status effects
Give enemies different attacks and behaviours Enemy tuning, champions, persistent defeats, enemy status effects
Create boss fights, waves and timed challenges Boss encounters, several bosses, encounter waves, combat trials, countdown courses
Give rewards and build an economy Collectibles and chests, loot tables, shops, banks, upgrade stations, offering altars, skill trainers
Offer equipment, character growth and power-ups Weapons, crafting, charms, random drops, leveling, power-ups
Give players quests and a current goal Quests, goals and Hint Stones
Record discoveries, achievements and completion Journal lore, bestiary, achievements, completion, play time and statistics, container fragments
Tell stories with people and scenes NPCs and dialogue, living towns, People journal, cutscenes and video
Shape a room's sound, light and atmosphere Sound sources, music, ambience, particles and camera, dark rooms, screen effects
Set the display, HUD, menu typography and controls UI theme and menu text, screen shapes, pixel scaling, controls and prompts
Offer accessibility choices and translated text Accessibility and assists, captions, translation, language fonts
Choose save rules, difficulty and another playthrough Save cards, copy and delete, difficulty and first-use hints, New Game+
Finish a game and unlock title-screen extras Preparation and endings, Extras gallery, jukebox and credits
Check progression, world size and gameplay before release Published world checks, large worlds, designer tools, testing tools, standalone release
Add optional Spine animation or Steam features Spine add-on, Steam achievements, presence and Cloud

Returning Creator Quick Reference#

After editing a room: save/export it in Tiled → stop the game → Prepare & Check → Build → Layout Editor: Rescan Rooms → (place any new room) → Export → Play Game.

What changed? What to do next
Room art, collision, objects, tileset, or room music Follow the room-edit loop above for every changed room.
Toolkit/editor scripts Reload Editor Scripts first, then follow the room-edit loop.
Room placement only Layout Editor ▸ Export, then Play Game.
One room's content Save the active map, then File → Export Current Room. In Defold, Prepare & Check → Build; the exported world placement stays the same.
Tiled world placement, membership, or room size File → Export All Maps and Tiled World, then Defold Prepare & Check → Build → Play Game. World workflow.
Movement or ability tuning Stop the game, use Project ▸ Game Settings… → Save and Prepare & Check, then Build and test. Published values override baseline config files.
A developer/config/ file edited by hand Prepare & Check → Build. If Game Settings or Prepare & Check says "Editor scripts changed", the editor still holds the old file: close Defold, start it again, run Reload Editor Scripts, then Prepare & Check → Build.
Enemy PNG frames or separate music files Prepare & Check syncs both; then Build.
Ready to distribute your game Follow the release steps.

Export choices: Tiled exports a room or all maps plus a world. Prepare & Check publishes a selected Tiled world; Layout Editor Export publishes manual placement. Defold Export Game creates the separate project you bundle for players. Build starts the game for testing.

1. Understand The Two Programs#

You will use two applications, each for a different job:

Program What you do there
Tiled Draw each room, paint collision and door markers, place ready-made templates, and export the room.
Defold Import art, generate playable rooms, validate the project, run the Layout Editor, and build/play the game.

The toolkit connects them. A normal room passes through this sequence:

Your .tmx room in Tiled
        ↓  Export Current Room
<room>.lua + <room>.tilemap
        ↓  Prepare & Check
Defold collection + factory + baked game objects
        ↓  Layout Editor Export
Published world layout used by Play Game

Essential words#

Word Plain-language meaning
Room One level map. One Tiled .tmx becomes one playable Defold room.
Tile One grid-sized piece of art, such as one 32 × 32 floor block.
Tilesheet A PNG containing many same-size tiles.
Tileset A Tiled .tsx file that tells Tiled how to cut and use a tilesheet.
Layer A named drawing/object surface inside a Tiled map. Layers have specific toolkit jobs.
Template A ready-made .tx object, such as enemy, checkpoint, or solid_box.
Property A setting attached to a map, layer, or object in Tiled's Properties panel.
Export Tiled creates the .lua and .tilemap files that the Defold tools consume.
Editor script A command added to Defold's Project menu. You click it; you do not write it.
Collection/factory Generated Defold resources used to load a playable room.
Layout Editor The in-game tool for positioning rooms and snapping matching doors.
Source A file you own and edit, such as .tmx, .tsx, PNG, WAV, or commented config.
Generated A file written by toolkit commands. Regenerate it instead of hand-editing it.

2. Install And Open The Toolkit#

Install the required versions#

  • Install Defold 1.14.1 from its release page, https://github.com/defold/defold/releases/tag/1.14.1. Later 1.14.x hotfixes are fine. (https://defold.com/download/ always offers the newest Defold, which may be a later version than this toolkit supports.)
  • Install Tiled 1.12.2, or a verified compatible Tiled 1.12.x build, from https://github.com/mapeditor/tiled/releases/tag/v1.12.2 or https://www.mapeditor.org/.

Put the toolkit where you will keep it#

Extract the entire toolkit zip to a writable folder, such as MyGame/Metroidvania_Toolkit. Do not work inside the zip viewer.

No separate toolkit installation is needed after extraction. Keep your Tiled maps in their own folder outside the toolkit, such as MyGame/Tiled Worlds, beside MyGame/Metroidvania_Toolkit.

Windows is the verified beta platform. macOS and Linux use the same steps but remain experimental.

  • Every platform: keep each map's Tile Layer Format at CSV. Rooms made with new_room_blank or Start a New World... already use it; for other maps, change it in Tiled's map properties. Compressed tile layer data needs Python 3 installed, and Zstandard compression is not supported.
  • macOS: install the Defold download matching your Mac (arm64 for Apple Silicon or x86_64 for Intel).
  • Linux: the verified target is an x86_64 glibc distribution, with Ubuntu 22.04 or later as the reference. Use Tiled's AppImage or a normal distribution package; Flatpak/Snap builds may use sandboxed paths and permissions and are not yet part of the verified setup. Install ImageMagick (sudo apt install imagemagick on Ubuntu) if you author picture objects or breakable background tiles from your own sheet.
Expected result: The toolkit folder contains game.project, MVTK.tiled-project, START_HERE.txt, START_HERE.html, developer/, mvtk/, tiled/, and docs/. START_HERE.html shows a picture of every set-up step.

Open Defold and set up the toolkit#

  1. Create MyGame/Tiled Worlds beside the toolkit folder if it does not exist.
  2. Start Defold.
  3. Click Open From Disk....
  4. Select the toolkit's root game.project.
  5. Wait until dependency loading and file indexing finish.
  6. Choose Project ▸ Set Up This Toolkit.
  7. Click Choose Maps Folder....
  8. Select MyGame/Tiled Worlds, the folder that will hold your world folders.

If the maps folder is already listed, skip choosing it and click Set Up. Use Change Maps Folder... to replace a listed folder. Set Up Without Folder lets you defer this choice. After Start a New World... in Tiled, Set Up can offer the worlds folder you picked there; click Set Up to accept it or Choose Another Folder... to replace it.

Set Up writes only inside the toolkit folder: it records where the toolkit is, lists your maps folder in Tiled's Project panel, and writes WHERE_THINGS_ARE.txt with the paths you open and export to. Listing the maps folder only makes it easy to find in Tiled; it does not import or export any maps. If Tiled already has the project open, open it again to show the new folder. Run Set Up again after moving or copying the toolkit folder.

Expected result: Defold's Assets pane shows developer, mvtk, and tiled, without a red error popup, and the toolkit folder contains WHERE_THINGS_ARE.txt. Open this HTML guide from your toolkit folder; documentation is excluded from Defold's asset loading.

The Assets pane is the project file tree. The Outline and Properties panes show the selected Defold resource; beginners rarely need them during room authoring. The Console at the bottom shows editor-command, validation, and game output. Keep it visible and treat the first red [ERROR] as the useful starting point when something fails. The toolkit commands used in this guide are under Defold's top Project menu.

Open Tiled#

  1. Start Tiled.
  2. Choose Edit ▸ Preferences... (on macOS it is in the Tiled menu) and click the Plugins tab.
  3. Tick defold and lua, then close the window. Tiled lists them by file name: defold.dll and lua.dll on Windows, libdefold.so and liblua.so on Linux. Every room export writes its .tilemap and .lua through these two plugins; defoldcollection is not needed. Tiled remembers the setting.
  4. Choose File ▸ Open File or Project.... (Tiled 1.12 has no Project ▸ Open Project item.)
  5. Open the toolkit folder, set the file type list at the bottom right to **Tiled Projects (\*.tiled-project)**, and open the root MVTK.tiled-project, not an individual room.
  6. If Tiled shows a yellow bar at the top right saying the project contains scripted extensions, click Enable Extensions. No bar appears when you enabled them before, or when the commands are installed in Tiled itself (see the optional step below).
Expected result: Tiled's File menu contains new_room_blank, Export Current Room, Export All Maps and Tiled World, Start a New World..., Save as Template Preset..., Add Connected Room..., Copy Room Properties to Rooms..., and Check Door Seams, and the Templates panel shows toolkit categories. The maps folder you chose in Defold appears in the Project panel.

Tiled exports to the toolkit whose MVTK.tiled-project is open. To work in another toolkit copy, open that copy's MVTK.tiled-project.

Then take one of these routes:

  • Your own game: first create a tileset for your art (section 3). It fixes your tile size and holds the collision shapes. Then File ▸ Start a New World... asks for a name, the first room's size and that tile size, and creates a world folder with one blank room in your maps folder. That room is not playable yet: section 4 uses it as your start room, adds two more rooms, then exports, prepares and plays them.
  • A sample pack (a separate download): unzip it outside the toolkit folder and follow the START_HERE.txt inside the pack instead. It starts from a fresh toolkit, one per pack. You copy the pack's developer folder into the toolkit by hand (with Defold closed, because it replaces some of the toolkit's settings and art), then open the pack's .world with File ▸ Open File or Project... where you unzipped it, exactly like a world of your own.

If the Templates panel is hidden, enable it from Tiled's View ▸ Views and Toolbars menu. If the toolkit File commands are absent, see Tiled toolkit menus or templates are missing.

Optional: the Tiled commands outside the toolkit project#

You do not need this when you open MVTK.tiled-project as described above. To also get the export commands when you open a room without the toolkit project, install them into Tiled's own extensions folder from the toolkit folder you work in:

  • Windows: run tools\set_tiled_export_path.bat.
  • macOS/Linux: in a terminal in the toolkit folder, run sh tools/set_tiled_export_path.sh.

Exports from rooms opened that way go to the toolkit where you last ran it.

Run one sanity build#

In Defold, choose Project ▸ Build or press Ctrl+B on Windows/Linux or Cmd+B on macOS. The empty toolkit should open at a title screen containing Play Game and Layout Editor. Player preferences are in Settings in the pause menu. Stop the game before editing or running editor commands.

Stop and fix: If the initial untouched project does not build, solve that installation problem before authoring rooms. Record the first red Console error; later errors are often consequences of the first one.

Starting over#

The toolkit release starts empty. To clear an existing project, use Project ▸ Reset Project to Empty (destructive) only when you intend to remove imported content, generated rooms, layout, music and saved settings. The command checks external originals and backs up removed content under developer/.trash/ before restoring the empty-project baseline.

3. Create Your First Tileset#

Choose the art and its real tile size before tuning Game Settings. Imported art establishes the project tile size.

Pick a simple first tilesheet#

Start with one PNG whose tiles are all the same size. Common sizes are 16 × 16, 32 × 32, or 64 × 64 pixels, but new_room_blank accepts any positive size. Square tiles are the safest beginner path because some validation and gameplay assumptions warn about non-square art.

You can use your own art or properly licensed third-party art. Keep the license and attribution information with your project.

Create the external .tsx#

In Tiled:

  1. Choose File ▸ New ▸ New Tileset….
  2. Choose Based on Tileset Image.
  3. Browse to the tilesheet PNG.
  4. Enter the PNG's exact Tile Width and Tile Height.
  5. Leave margin and spacing at zero unless the art pack explicitly uses them.
  6. Save the tileset as an external .tsx beside your source art or in your authoring folder.
Expected result: A Tileset editor opens and displays individual selectable tiles rather than one undivided image.

Import single images (Collection of Images tileset)#

Use this when some of your art is separate PNG files — one file per prop, decoration, or background piece — rather than packed into one gridded sheet. Tiled calls this a Collection of Images tileset, and the toolkit imports each image for you.

  1. In Tiled, choose File ▸ New ▸ New Tileset….
  2. Set Type to Collection of Images (instead of Based on Tileset Image).
  3. Name the tileset and save it as an external .tsx beside your art or in your authoring folder.
  4. With the tileset open in the Tileset editor, click the Add Tiles (+) button and select one or more PNGs. Each image becomes one tile you can place. The images may be different sizes.
  5. Save the .tsx with Ctrl+S.

Add this tileset to a room the same way as a gridded one — Map ▸ Add External Tileset…. Select the art/background object layer, select an image in the Tilesets panel, choose the Insert Tile tool (T), and click the canvas to place the image as a tile object. Do not paint collection images onto art/environment; that is a tile layer reserved for the room's primary gridded tilesheet.

When you run Export Current Room, the toolkit copies every image in the collection into developer/assets/tiled/backgrounds/ and relinks the exported room and tileset to those project-local copies. Prepare & Check then consumes the localized assets. You do not move the files yourself.

Important: A room's painted tile layers still use one primary gridded tilesheet for its floors and walls (see the collision note below). A Collection of Images tileset, image layers, and image-collection object art are additional art that coexists with that primary sheet — they are not a replacement for it, and single images are not where you paint tile collision on the room's gridded layers.

For keys, save points, chests, NPCs and other gameplay objects, use the Tiled object artwork workflow. Draw the interaction area with the rectangle template and link collection-image objects using owner_ref, or place a single image object with its own behavior. That guide also covers catalog artwork for drops, optional state images, and interaction particles.

Torch artwork uses default when unlit and lit when lit. Checkpoint artwork uses active only while it is the current respawn point, and default otherwise. Another checkpoint, save point or fast travel station can replace that point. Room loading and save reloading restore these pictures silently. Without a linked lit or active image, the default image stays unchanged.

Make a torch or checkpoint change its look#

  1. In Tiled, select the top-level templates object layer.
  2. Place Interaction ▸ Torch or Navigation ▸ Checkpoint from the Templates panel.
  3. Select your unlit torch or inactive checkpoint image in the Tilesets panel.
  4. Choose Insert Tile (T).
  5. Click the canvas to place the image.
  6. Set the image object's Class to object_art.
  7. Select the torch or checkpoint with the image's owner_ref object picker.
  8. Set the image's art_state to default.
  9. Select your lit torch or active checkpoint image in the Tilesets panel.
  10. Place that image with Insert Tile at the same position.
  11. Set the second image's Class to object_art.
  12. Select the same torch or checkpoint with its owner_ref object picker.
  13. Set its art_state to lit for a torch or active for a checkpoint.
  14. Save the room with Ctrl+S.
  15. Choose File ▸ Export Current Room.
  16. In Defold, choose Project ▸ Reload Editor Scripts.
  17. Choose Project ▸ Prepare & Check.
  18. Choose Project ▸ Build.
  19. Choose Play Game on the title screen.
  20. Light the torch or touch the checkpoint to check its alternate image.

On a Torch, ignition ▸ allow_extinguish lets interaction return to the default image; ignition ▸ starts_lit starts on the lit image. ignition ▸ set_flag remembers that the torch was lit after saving, as before; extinguishing does not clear that flag. A Checkpoint's activation ▸ trigger_once limits activation during the session; it does not keep the active picture after another respawn point becomes current.

Add tile collision shapes#

Collision comes from the Tile Collision Editor shapes of the tiles you paint, on any tile layer that does not scroll, including environment, background_*, foreground_*, and solid. Layer names and visibility do not add or remove those shapes. The grapple and ice layers are tags and add no terrain collision; world-state layers can opt out with state_collision: none.

Scrolling (parallax) layers never collide. A layer scrolls when its Parallax X or Y, multiplied by every parent group's factor, differs from 1. A parallax_visual marker or a listing in parallax_settings.lua alone does not make a factor-1 layer scroll. Prepare & Check warns when scrolling layers use tiles with collision shapes.

The solid layer is optional: for invisible collision, paint shaped tiles there and hide the layer. For decoration, use tiles without collision shapes, including on factor-1 foreground and background layers. Collision is not inferred from tile numbers or visible pixels.

  1. Open the external tileset for editing.
  2. Select a tile that should be solid.
  3. In the Tileset editor, switch to Tile Collision Editor. If that view is hidden, enable it from View ▸ Views and Toolbars while the .tsx is open.
  4. Choose the rectangle or polygon tool and draw around the solid part of the tile.
  5. Repeat for every solid tile you plan to paint.
  6. Save the .tsx with Ctrl+S.

The toolkit exporter rejects unsaved referenced tilesets because exporting stale collision would produce a room that looks correct but plays incorrectly.

For simple invisible blocks or slopes, you may instead place the solid_box or solid_slope template later. Those are object-based collision shapes.

4. Build A Three-Room Starter World In Tiled#

This walkthrough makes three horizontal rooms:

start_room  --right/left--  middle_room  --right/left--  goal_room

Create start_room#

You need the tileset from section 3 before you paint. Make it first if you skipped that section.

If you used File ▸ Start a New World..., its blank room rooms/<name>_01.tmx is your start room and is already open. Do not create another one: skip to Add your external tileset to the room, and read <name>_01 wherever this walkthrough says start_room.

Otherwise, create the start room now:

  1. In Tiled, choose File ▸ new_room_blank.
  2. Enter a room size in tiles, such as 40x23.
  3. Enter your tilesheet's pixel tile size, such as 32 or 32x32.
  4. Save the map as start_room.tmx in your maps folder.

Save originals outside the Defold project, on any accessible drive. The folders under developer/assets/ contain disposable exported copies. Export rejects original maps, tilesets, images, and file-property assets inside the project.

Use a simple filename matching this pattern:

[A-Za-z0-9_][A-Za-z0-9_-]*

That means letters, digits, or underscore first; letters, digits, underscore, or hyphen afterward. Avoid spaces for beginner projects.

Expected result: Tiled creates a map with top-level bounds, grapple, solid, and templates layers; an exit group containing door_left, door_right, door_top, and door_bottom; and an art group containing the visual layers including environment.

Do not rename or remove the generated structural layers.

Add your external tileset to the room#

With start_room.tmx open, choose Map ▸ Add External Tileset… and select the .tsx you created. The exact wording can vary slightly across Tiled 1.12.x, but the result must be the external tileset visible in the room's Tilesets panel.

Know the Tiled workspace#

Before painting, identify these five areas:

Area What it is for
Center canvas The room you paint and the objects you position.
Layers panel Selects whether you are painting art, bounds, collision, doors, or placing templates. Always check the selected layer before editing.
Tilesets panel Shows tiles from the external .tsx; click a tile here before painting the canvas.
Templates panel Holds the ready-made gameplay objects you drag onto the top-level templates layer.
Properties panel Shows settings for the selected map, layer, tile, or template object. Click the item first, then edit its property here.

Use View ▸ Views and Toolbars to restore a hidden panel. The most common beginner mistake is painting or dragging while the wrong layer is selected, so confirm the Layers highlight before every new kind of edit.

Room layers at a glance#

new_room_blank creates these layers for you. Do not rename or delete them.

Layer Job
bounds The room's outer footprint. Paint a closed one-tile ring; keep it unbroken even at doorways.
solid (optional) Invisible collision. Paint tiles with Tile Collision Editor shapes here and hide the layer; keep Parallax X / Y at 1.
grapple Hook tags. Paint the ceiling's bottom rock row or hanging anchor cells; give them visible art. The tags are hidden in game and bake into editable Grapple Surface gameobjects.
ice (optional) Paint over floor cells for slippery ground. Hidden tags bake into editable Ice Floor strips; keep the solid floor underneath and Parallax X / Y at 1.
art/environment Visible floors and walls use tiles with collision shapes. Decoration uses tiles without collision shapes. Any layer at Parallax 1 keeps its tiles' shapes; scrolling layers never collide.
exit/door_left, door_right, door_top, door_bottom Exit markers. One painted tile shows the Layout Editor where this room connects.
templates (top level) Where you drag ready-made objects — player start, enemies, collectibles, hazards, NPCs, and so on.

Painting on the wrong layer is the most common first-world mistake. Confirm the highlighted layer in the Layers panel before each new kind of edit.

Paint visible art, bounds, and collision#

Paint in this order so each layer has one clear job:

  1. Select art/environment and paint the visible floor and walls with tiles that have Tile Collision Editor shapes. Use tiles without collision shapes for decoration.
  2. Select bounds and paint a one-tile outer room outline.
  3. If the room uses grappling, paint a grapple layer where the hook may catch. Use visible hooks, rings or rails on the art layers to mark these spots. See Grappling for tagged surfaces and the Game Settings Grapple tab.

Use tiles without collision shapes for foreground decoration. A foreground layer at Parallax 1 collides wherever its tiles have shapes; scrolling layers never collide. For invisible collision only, paint shaped tiles on the optional solid layer and hide it. Visible shaped floor tiles already collide.

Expected result: The room looks like a small platforming space, bounds outlines it, and shaped tiles on layers that do not scroll block the player. The optional hidden solid layer supplies any invisible collision.

Let painted Tiled art replace a template placeholder#

Player starts, checkpoints, and save points are invisible gameplay markers. Paint any artwork for them on Tiled's visible art layers. They never add a Defold placeholder sprite, even when an older map still has use_defold_sprite=true.

Some gameplay templates include a simple Defold sprite so the object remains visible before final art exists. When you paint the finished object art on a visible Tiled tile layer, select the template object and set use_defold_sprite=false. Prepare & Check then keeps the object's scripts, collision, effects, and behavior while omitting only its embedded Defold component named sprite.

Leave use_defold_sprite=true when the Defold prefab should draw the object. Platforms and volumes that normally expect painted art—one-way platforms, gates, breakable walls, pressure plates, water volumes, bounce pads, conveyors, timed doors, and wall-jump surfaces—default to false; the other supported templates default to true. Water distortion remains available because it is a separate effect, not the placeholder sprite.

After changing the property, save and export the room, then run Prepare & Check. Validation warns when the placeholder is disabled but no visible non-control tile is painted beneath the object.

For example, to replace a teleporter placeholder, paint the teleporter art on a visible tile layer directly beneath the teleporter template object, set that object's use_defold_sprite property to false, export, and run the three commands above. Set the property back to true whenever you want the prefab's built-in teleporter sprite again.

Place the player start#

  1. Select the visible top-level templates object layer.
  2. In the Templates panel, open Navigation.
  3. Drag player_start into a safe open point above the floor.
  4. Leave its defaults (spawn_type=player, is_default=true, and checkpoint_id=start) for this first world.

For the Layout Editor route, one player_start is enough to identify the starting room. Export All Maps and Tiled World also needs the boolean map property is_starting_room=true on exactly one room of the world: Start a New World... sets it on its first room; for another world, add it in the start room's Map Properties. Avoid multiple starting rooms because selection can then depend on ordering or saved state.

Paint the first door marker#

Select exit/door_right and paint at least one tile where the right exit should connect. Door connectivity comes from these painted door_* layers, not from a door template and not from the solid layer.

Leave a real opening in visible/collision art so the player can pass through the edge. A painted door marker tells Layout Editor where rooms connect; it does not automatically carve a hole through your wall art.

Add optional room music#

  1. Choose Map ▸ Map Properties.
  2. Find or add music as a file property.
  3. Select an Ogg Vorbis .ogg or WAV .wav file.

The file may be outside the toolkit. Prepare & Check copies and registers it. Empty music means “keep the current track.” Adjacent rooms using the same file continue the song instead of restarting it.

Defold does not use MP3 for this workflow. Convert MP3, FLAC, M4A, AAC, or Opus to .ogg or .wav first. For example:

ffmpeg -i song.mp3 song.ogg

The biome property names the room's region. A room without its own music plays its region's music when developer/config/biomes.lua gives the region one (see Give a region its own music).

Save and export start_room#

  1. Save the map with Ctrl+S.
  2. Save the .tsx too if you edited collision.
  3. Choose File ▸ Export Current Room.
  4. Read the export summary and correct any error before continuing.
Expected result: The project contains developer/assets/tiled/maps/start_room.lua and developer/assets/tiled/maps/start_room.tilemap.

Create middle_room#

Rooms two and three are new maps. Create each with File ▸ new_room_blank (also when your first room came from Start a New World) and save it in the same folder as the start room:

  1. Use the same tile size and add the same external .tsx.
  2. Paint shaped floor and wall tiles on environment, then paint bounds.
  3. Do not add another player_start.
  4. Paint one tile on door_left and one on door_right.
  5. Add one easy recipe object, such as checkpoint, collectible, enemy, or sign, on the top-level templates layer.
  6. Save as middle_room.tmx and export it.

Create goal_room#

Repeat once more:

  1. Use the same tile size and add the same external .tsx.
  2. Paint environment, bounds, and collision.
  3. Paint one door_left marker.
  4. Add a save_point, chest, or other simple reward.
  5. Save as goal_room.tmx and export it.
Expected result: All three rooms have matching horizontal doors: start_room right ↔ middle_room left, and middle_room right ↔ goal_room left.
Started with Start a New World? New rooms are not added to its world automatically, and this walkthrough does not need them there: you export each room and arrange the rooms in the Layout Editor (section 7). To keep every room in the Tiled world instead, open the start room, select the World Tool on Tiled's toolbar, click Add another map to the current world, choose the new room, and drag it beside its neighbor so the doors line up. Then follow Use a world already arranged in Tiled.

Add connected rooms to a Tiled world#

When your rooms live in a Tiled world, Tiled can build the next room beside one you have open. The new room gets a closed bounds ring, the same region (biome), darkness, room_zone, music and combat music, and door cells that line up with door cells painted in the open room. Both rooms use the open room's own bounds and door tiles. Nothing else changes: the open room's bounds ring and art stay as they are, and the new room is never the starting room.

  1. Open a room that belongs to the world.
  2. Choose File ▸ Add Connected Room....
  3. In Side, choose the edge the new room joins.
  4. Check the width, height, door span, door offset and file name. The door fields start from a door already painted on that side, if there is one.
  5. Click OK.
  6. Paint the new room's art and floor, using tiles with collision shapes for the floor on a layer that does not scroll.
  7. Choose File ▸ Check Door Seams.
Expected result: The new room opens beside the first room in the World view, its door cells touch the first room's door cells, and Check Door Seams lists no problem for the two rooms.

The command refuses rather than guesses: the open room's bounds ring must be closed and lie on the map edge on that side, its tilesets must be external .tsx files, the new room must not overlap another room, and it must be saved outside the toolkit folder. Leave the doorway clear of shaped tiles on every layer that does not scroll, including the optional solid layer. If a solid tile covers the new door cells in the open room, it says so; erase that tile yourself. Check Door Seams reads the saved files of every room in the world and reports unmatched doors, doors whose cells miss each other, and overlapping rooms, in the same words as Prepare & Check, which stays the final check.

Copy room properties to other rooms of a Tiled world#

Tiled can copy the region (biome), darkness, room_zone, music and combat music of the room you have open to other rooms of the same world. It picks the rooms by part of their file name and lists them before anything changes. It never copies room_title or room_title_card, which name one room.

  1. Open the room whose properties you want to copy.
  2. Choose File ▸ Copy Room Properties to Rooms....
  3. Tick each property to copy.
  4. In File name contains, type part of the target rooms' file names. Leave it empty for every other room of the world.
  5. Click OK.
  6. Read the list of rooms and values.
  7. Click Yes.
  8. Choose File ▸ Export All Maps and Tiled World.
Expected result: Each listed room's Map Properties show the copied values, and the rooms you did not list are unchanged.

Rooms open in Tiled are changed in their tab and saved, together with any other unsaved edits in them; the list names those rooms first. Other rooms are read from disk, changed and saved. A property the open room does not have is not copied, and the target keeps its own value. The command refuses a read-only .world file.

Author rooms outside the toolkit folder#

Keep original .tmx, .tsx, .world, tilesheet PNG, image layers, and music in your own art/level folder outside the Defold project, including on another drive.

  1. Open MVTK.tiled-project first so the toolkit menus and templates are loaded.
  2. Open or create the external room.
  3. Keep editing the original external files.
  4. Save the map and every referenced .tsx.
  5. Run File ▸ Export Current Room after each change.

If the room uses another toolkit's templates, Export Current Room offers to use this toolkit's matching templates. Export All Maps and Tiled World offers one confirmation for all its rooms. Presets keep their original references.

  1. Review the old references and new targets in Use this toolkit's templates?.
  2. Click Yes to update the matched references in the saved original maps.
  3. Check the backup beside each changed map: <room>.tmx.mvtk-bak (or .mvtk-bak-2, .mvtk-bak-3, and so on when a backup already exists).

Click No to keep the original references and export with the copy-and-warn behaviour described below. Unmatched references also keep that behaviour. Save a modified room before accepting a world retarget. Quiet batch exports do not offer or perform retargeting.

To undo a retarget:

  1. Close the room's tab in Tiled.
  2. Move the rewritten <room>.tmx aside.
  3. Rename the chosen <room>.tmx.mvtk-bak (or numbered backup) to <room>.tmx.
  4. Reopen the restored room in Tiled.

The exporter creates project-local copies:

External source Project-local destination
Localized map copy developer/assets/tiled/maps/<room>.tmx
Generated room data developer/assets/tiled/maps/<room>.lua
Generated Defold tilemap developer/assets/tiled/maps/<room>.tilemap
External .tsx developer/assets/tiled/tilesets/
Single-sheet tileset image developer/assets/tilesheets/
Image-collection and image-layer art developer/assets/tiled/backgrounds/
Room music after Prepare & Check developer/assets/music/

The localized .tmx contains an mvtk-localized-from provenance comment. It lets later exports recognize the original source and protects unrelated same-named project maps from silent overwrite.

Export also writes .mvtk-source receipts beside copied maps and assets. Reset Project checks that originals are readable outside the project and that copies have not been edited since export. Missing receipts, unavailable originals, or changed copies stop reset before any files move. Reconnect the source drive or preserve local edits in the external originals, then export again. The toolkit's shipped tiled/templates/ files remain framework-owned and survive reset.

Important: Never edit the localized .tmx, .tsx, or copied image inside the toolkit when the original lives elsewhere. Edit the original source and export again. External .tx templates are copied into developer/assets/tiled/templates/external/ with an export warning. Keep editing the original template and export again. Templates with a tile must come from the shipped tiled/templates/. Enemy art file properties are localized with their skin assets; remove other non-empty file properties or use a shipped template before exporting.

5. Turn Tiled Exports Into Playable Defold Rooms#

Stop the running game, then run these commands in this exact order:

  1. Project ▸ Reload Editor Scripts
  2. Project ▸ Prepare & Check
  3. Project ▸ Build

What each command does#

Command Purpose
Reload Editor Scripts Loads editor commands after installation or an update. Changed Lua modules can require restarting Defold first.
Prepare & Check Applies saved player settings, imports changed assets, creates rooms, synchronizes enemy art/music, validates the result and checks the world budget. It also publishes placement when Tiled owns the world.
Preparation Status Explains what needs exporting, preparing, publishing or rebuilding.
Build Compiles and launches the prepared project.

The Console still names the internal Import Assets, Create Rooms and Validate All stages. They run in the required order inside Prepare & Check; they are no longer separate menu choices.

Watch the Defold Console. Fix the first red error before moving on.

For within-room connections, use Tiled's optional target_ref, boss_ref, and arena_ref object pickers. String IDs still support cross-room links and switches with several listeners. See Connecting authored objects for supported targets, diagnostic navigation, and freshness warnings.

Tiled export, Prepare & Check, Build, Layout Export, and Export Game check authored relationships. Blocking errors preserve the previous published outputs; warnings identify facts that still need review. Prepare & Check refresh the project index used by Tiled. After a toolkit update, run Project ▸ Set Up This Toolkit and restart Tiled to load the new extension.

After a toolkit upgrade, run Prepare & Check again. Room resources load ahead of entry; startup, respawn and fast travel wait until the destination is ready. If loading fails, the game reports it instead of moving the player into an empty room. Advanced load limits are commented in developer/config/stream_settings.lua.

Expected result: The Console names all three rooms, reports imported tiles/resources and generated rooms, Prepare & Check succeeds, and Build opens the title screen.

Important generated outputs include:

  • developer/rooms/collections/<room>.collection
  • developer/rooms/factories/<room>_factory.collectionfactory
  • developer/rooms/generated/
  • developer/config/rooms_config.lua
  • mvtk/editor_host/rooms_manifest.collection
  • developer/config/tile_settings.lua

Do not hand-edit those to fix an authoring problem. Correct the Tiled source or creator config, then regenerate.

6. Tune The Player With Game Settings#

Stop the game, then choose Project ▸ Game Settings… in Defold. Prepare & Check should already have read your real tilesheet and generated developer/config/tile_settings.lua. Imported art owns tile size; Game Settings does not choose it or create rooms.

  1. On Player, choose Current, Balanced, Fast or Heavy in the Preset dropdown, then click Apply preset. Current restores saved values; the other presets change only the draft.
  2. Leave Player size (1 = 100%) at 1.00 for art drawn at its in-game size. The numeric field accepts sizes from 1/7 to 8. With Scale movement enabled, the runtime scales world-pixel movement and traversal values once.
  3. On Abilities, check what the player should own when a new game begins. Unchecked abilities stay locked until a matching pickup is collected. Advanced and Grapple values apply whenever the ability becomes available, including restored saves and checkpoints; starting checkboxes do not disable that tuning.
  4. Review the settings you need on Advanced, Combat, Combat features, Grapple and Game. Hover over a name or field to read its help.
  5. Use Player art, Ending, Camera, HUD and prompts, Map, Saves and checkpoints, or Movement test for those tasks. These tabs share the same draft. Designer tools explains imports and previews.
  6. Click Save and Prepare & Check, finish the preparation prompts, and close the window when preparation completes. Choose Project ▸ Build to play with the saved settings. Save on its own publishes settings without running preparation.

Hold Up + Light attack to hit above the player on the ground or in the air. Shots follow the movement stick in eight directions by default. Neutral input shoots toward the facing direction; downward shots require being airborne by default. Up near a ladder still climbs, so check your room's ladder placement.

  1. Select Combat features ▸ Directional attacks ▸ Up attacks to enable upward attacks.
  2. Choose Aim ranged attack: eight, four, or facing for straight shots.
  3. Enable Aim stance to hold Ranged on the ground while aiming and fire on release (off by default).
  4. Set Combat ▸ Up attack ▸ Up attack damage to tune upward hits.
  5. Click Save and Prepare & Check.
  6. Choose Project ▸ Build.
  7. Choose Play Game on an empty save slot.
  8. Test an up attack against a flyer while standing on the ground.
  9. Test an up attack against a flyer in the air.
  10. Test shots with the keyboard in all eight directions while airborne.
  11. Test shots with a controller stick in all eight directions while airborne.
  12. Hold Ranged on the ground to stop walking and aim with the stick.
  13. Release Ranged to fire toward the last held aim.
  14. Test Down + Ranged with Bomb unlocked to place a bomb.

The commented developer/config/combat_config.lua also tunes the upward-input threshold, aim deadzone and grounded downward shots. Up attacks use the existing weapon attack clip; optional shot aim clips use the existing animation fallback. Aim stance leaves vertical input and external motion available. It does not activate during Compact Form, stun, climbing or hanging. With Aim stance off, Ranged fires on press as before. Settings edits require Prepare & Check again before Export Game.

Cut and reflect enemy shots#

Both projectile counterplay features start off. With Cut projectiles on, player melee swings break enemy shots by default. Each Enemy can instead ignore swings or return its shots toward the shooter. Returned shots use the player's damage path, including combat energy gains and boss stagger.

  1. Open Project ▸ Game Settings… in Defold.
  2. Tick Combat features ▸ Projectiles ▸ Cut projectiles.
  3. Tick Combat features ▸ Parry ▸ Parry reflects shots if perfect parries should return reflectable shots.
  4. Click Save and Prepare & Check.
  5. Select a shooting Enemy in Tiled.
  6. Expand Tuning.
  7. Set Projectile deflect to reflect to return its shots with a swing or perfect parry.
  8. Set Reflect damage mult to a nonnegative multiplier, or leave -1 to inherit.
  9. Choose File ▸ Export Current Room.
  10. Choose Project ▸ Prepare & Check in Defold.
  11. Choose Project ▸ Build.
  12. Open Layout Editor from the title screen.
  13. Click Export.
  14. Choose Play Game on an empty save slot.
  15. Hit an enemy shot with a light attack to check the effect and returned damage.
  16. Perfect-parry a reflectable shot to check its return.

Projectile deflect inherit uses the archetype, then enemy_defaults.lua (default destroy). none prevents cuts and returns. destroy allows cuts without reflection. Parry reflects shots also requires Parry and Perfect parry. Boss projectile patterns opt in with projectile_deflect="reflect" in developer/config/attack_patterns.lua. An archetype can set the same two fields in developer/config/enemy_types.lua. The commented combat_config.lua projectiles block sets feedback ids, returned speed, fallback damage and lifetime. Individual player melee attacks can set cuts_projectiles=false. Player shots and movement contacts do not cut. With both features off, shots and parries retain their existing behaviour. Feel and timing need checking in your running game. Run Prepare & Check again after settings changes before Export Game.

Parry tells are optional and start off. Their default cyan differs from the Shield guard's white flash and the spike shell's amber warning.

  1. Open Project ▸ Game Settings… in Defold.
  2. Tick Combat features ▸ Parry ▸ Parry tells.
  3. Click Save and Prepare & Check.
  4. Reopen the Tiled project to refresh Enemy tuning.
  5. Select an Enemy with a melee_swipe attack in Tiled.
  6. Expand Tuning.
  7. Tick Attack parryable.
  8. Choose File ▸ Export Current Room.
  9. Choose Project ▸ Prepare & Check in Defold.
  10. Choose Project ▸ Build.
  11. Open Layout Editor from the title screen.
  12. Click Export.
  13. Choose Play Game on an empty save slot.
  14. Parry the swipe after its cyan windup flash.
  15. Check the colours against your art in every display mode and colour filter.

Attack parryable allows melee swipe parries even with tells off. Unticked inherits attack_parryable from developer/config/enemy_types.lua; every shipped preset is false. Ticking it opts one placement in. To disable a preset's opt-in, set that preset to false; an unticked placement cannot override a true preset. This field never changes contact damage or the existing shot parry rules. Parryable enemy shots receive the same colour when spawned; player shots and returned shots keep their own art. Per-shot material tint breaks sprite batching and can add draw calls.

The fully commented feedback.parry_tell in developer/config/combat_config.lua sets RGBA colour, flash duration and an optional sound_bank.lua sound id. feedback.unblockable_tell.enabled opts into a separate warning on non-parryable windups; it starts false. Boss pattern overrides in developer/config/attack_patterns.lua can set parryable=true or false to classify their telegraph. Projectile, area, beam, charge, grab, mine and wave telegraphs inherit their existing parryable default when the field is omitted. Set the field explicitly on melee or compound patterns whose telegraph needs a cue. An explicit telegraph_color always wins. The boss also flashes through its animation controller when its tell supplies the colour.

The existing anim.flash eases back to white. An enemy's preset tint is lost after a tell, as after a hurt flash; this visual behaviour remains UNVERIFIED in engine. Check the colours and animation in your running game. Run Prepare & Check again before Export Game after changing settings.

Air Dash follows eight stick directions by default and pauses gravity, so a sideways dash flies flat. Upward dashes reach higher ledges; playtest existing routes after changing these settings.

  1. Choose Advanced ▸ Air Dash ▸ Air dash directions: eight follows the stick; horizontal preserves horizontal dashes and vertical momentum.
  2. Set Pause gravity while dashing to hold vertical speed in eight mode.
  3. Set Extra dashes per upgrade to the charges added by Extra Air Dash.
  4. Check Abilities ▸ Movement ▸ Extra air dash for a starting upgrade.

For a pickup instead, place ability_extra_air_dash in Tiled. Air Dash itself must also be unlocked. A mid-air pickup refills dashes to the upgraded maximum.

Super Jump charges while holding Down and standing still on the ground, then launches straight up when Jump is pressed. The launch uses base jump height; High Jump does not multiply it. Releasing Down keeps the charge briefly.

  1. Check Abilities ▸ Movement ▸ Super Jump to start new games with it.
  2. Set Advanced ▸ Super Jump ▸ Charge time (seconds) to the hold duration.
  3. Set Launch height (x normal) to the height multiplier.
  4. Click Save and Prepare & Check.
  5. Choose Project ▸ Build.

For a pickup instead, place ability_super_jump in Tiled. For a ceiling block, place a Breakable Wall with Required ability set to super_jump. The block remains an editable gameobject in the baked room collection. Down+Jump on a one-way platform still drops through when the charge is not ready. Climbing, swimming and attacks prevent charging; ladder-top jumps keep their ordinary behavior. Test the charge feel, shaft height and ceiling breaking in play.

Blink uses a second Dash press during an air dash or a ground slide. It can cross spikes and enemies, but walls, gates, platforms and room boundaries shorten or refuse the teleport. It is unavailable while swimming or climbing.

  1. Check Abilities ▸ Movement ▸ Blink to start new games with Blink.
  2. Set Advanced ▸ Blink ▸ Blink distance (px) to the maximum distance.
  3. Set Second-press window (s) to the grace after a dash or slide ends.
  4. Click Save and Prepare & Check.
  5. Choose Project ▸ Build.

For a pickup instead, place ability_blink in Tiled. Air Dash or Slide must also be unlocked. The second press uses the player's remapped Dash button.

The name of a field is blue when it differs from the toolkit default. Its x button restores that default; Save publishes the reset. Values outside the allowed range show an error on the field and block Save. Dependent fields are disabled when they do not apply; hover over them for the reason. Enter confirms a typed value without closing the window.

The header counts settings that differ from defaults and unsaved changes. Close with an unsaved draft offers Discard or Keep editing. Keep editing retains the draft and reopens on Player. Import, preview and line-editor actions also return to Player; this is a native tab-selection limitation.

Game name and ID: on the Game tab, give the game its own name. Save writes it into game.project. A toolkit placeholder name shows a warning on the field without blocking Save. The first Prepare & Check (or Save) quietly assigns a permanent ID, shown in the read-only Game ID row. Renaming the title keeps the ID and Continue data. For a project copy intended as a different game, clear game_id in the copy's developer/config/persistence_settings.lua, then run Prepare & Check once. The window does not copy older player data or import machine-local authoring settings; beta saves are disposable. Compatibility details.

Game Settings publishes the commented developer/config/creator_settings.lua. Player, starting-ability and combat configuration files remain the authored baseline. Published overrides travel with the project and exported game. Player preferences and save-slot progress remain separate; change player preferences through Settings in the game's pause menu.

On Game, Death drop is off by default: death restores inventory and currency from the last save. When enabled, Currency dropped sets the loss fraction and Minimum dropped sets a minimum, capped by the saved amount. The amount fields are disabled while Death drop is off. A new drop replaces an uncollected one. See the death-and-saves recipe for placement and persistence.

On Game ▸ When the game loses focus, Pause on focus loss and Pause when the controller disconnects start on; Mute in the background starts off. Players override them in Pause ▸ Settings under Graphics, Controls and Audio, respectively. Focus loss or minimise opens Pause during play; returning never resumes automatically. An Items-only Inventory or world lore popup closes first. Unplugging an unused controller does nothing. Background mute preserves saved volumes and mute, and clears on return or Quit to Menu. The title screen and Layout Editor are excluded.

The Grapple tab selects Swing or Pull, hook targets, Stay hooked, rope extension, range, power, speed and release behaviour. Hook speed, right after Range under Swing and rope, sets flight speed in base px/s and scales with Player size. 0 catches instantly, as before. A positive speed draws the flying rope tip; the catch sound, stamina cost and cooldown happen only at attachment. A target that moves away or becomes blocked is missed for free. During flight, release Grapple to cancel with Stay hooked off; with Stay hooked or the player's Grapple toggle on, press again to cancel. Jump does not cancel flight. For an optional let-go pose, use Player art to import a grapple_release_0001.png sequence or map grapple_release=<clip>; a Spine player needs a grapple_release entry in developer/config/player_spine_map.lua. Click Save and Prepare & Check, then Build. The pose shows only after a player let-go, in jump or fall while carry time remains. Cannon launches, impacts, hang jumps, room exits and lost anchors do not use it. Without the clip or Spine mapping, the usual jump/fall pose remains. Pull hang keeps you at the hook in Pull style. Pump with swing only, Pump start speed and Counter-swing brake control pumping in Swing style; each has hover help and a reset (x). Use Camera ▸ Launch room preview to try the unsaved draft in a copy of your published rooms. See Grappling.

Changing Player size adjusts supported resources while retaining custom atlas, prototype, animation, collision-mask and material references. Main body geometry uses the authored collider half-width and half-height. Other collider geometry and the Spine visual child's proportions scale with Player size. Supported resources include one embedded sprite, the existing spine_visual collection instance, and box, sphere and capsule collision shapes. Ambiguous or unsupported structures stop the save with the affected file named; existing files remain intact. External sprite components and arbitrary multi-shape main bodies need asset-tool support before they can be scaled automatically.

The generated player_resources_generated.lua records previous dimensions so repeated saves and Prepare & Check do not compound scale. Keep it with the project. For an existing custom player, save its current size once in Game Settings before directly changing the baseline size. Reset Project and empty toolkit packaging restore baseline geometry before clearing project overrides.

The window stays open after Save and shows the result. Opening Game Settings or running Prepare & Check recovers an interrupted project transaction. If an interrupted save prevents a build, restart Defold, run Reload Editor Scripts, then Prepare & Check. Preparation remains the owner of tile_settings.lua.

Use the optional Spine add-on#

Spine is off in a fresh toolkit. Sprite games need no Spine runtime or resources. Enable the add-on only for a Spine player or boss. Everyone building with it needs their own Spine Editor license (Essential or higher); the free Trial does not cover the runtime. See the Spine Editor License Agreement.

  1. Stop the game. Choose Project ▸ Spine Add-on…, then Turn On.
  2. Read the license notice and confirm Turn On. Close the completion message and wait for the command to fetch libraries. If it instead asks for Project ▸ Fetch Libraries, follow that instruction.
  3. Reopen Defold if it has not picked up the .defignore change. Run Project ▸ Prepare & Check before Build or Export Game.

Turning it on leaves your chosen player unchanged. For the bundled Spine player, set player.player_type = "spine" in developer/config/game_config.lua. Published overrides in developer/config/creator_settings.lua take priority. A sprite player can share a game with Spine bosses. The Spine add-on guide explains how to add a boss component to boss_runtime.go, register it in boss_visuals.lua, and select it through the boss preset or Tiled properties. Keep the rig's resources under developer/addons/spine/bosses/<your_boss>/; placed Tiled bosses remain editable gameobject instances baked into the room collection.

The bundled player's resources now live together under developer/addons/spine/player/: the rig/ folder, player_spine_visual.go, player.collection and player_spine.collection. Keep these together when updating the bundled player; mixing versions changes its displayed size. Preserve your own custom geometry and developer/config/player_resources_generated.lua scale history. Game Settings saves and Prepare & Check keep both add-on collections and the core bone colliders in sync with Player Size, even while Spine is off. A replacement rig also needs matching animation mappings and bone names; the add-on guide lists the configuration files.

To turn it off, select a sprite player and replace Spine bosses with sprites. Export the changed Tiled rooms and run Prepare & Check while Spine is still on, so baked collections use the new selections. Remove Spine component and resource references outside the add-on folder, including any explicit add-on custom-resource declaration in game.project. Choose Project ▸ Spine Add-on…, then Turn Off. The command names any remaining files or settings to change and refuses until they are clear. Let it refresh libraries, reopen Defold if requested, then run Prepare & Check before Build or Export Game. The add-on files remain available for later use. If the dialog reports Mixed, choose Turn On or Turn Off again to repair the project state.

Use the optional Steam add-on#

The Steam add-on ships off. Follow the Steam add-on guide for setup through Project ▸ Steam Add-on…, achievement mapping, rich presence and Steam Cloud.

7. Arrange And Publish The World In Layout Editor#

Use a world already arranged in Tiled#

Layout Editor is optional when you arrange the rooms in a saved Tiled .world.

  1. Open the world in Tiled. Use finite orthogonal TMX maps with the project's tile dimensions, matching painted door openings, and exactly one starting room: the room with the player_start, marked with the boolean map property is_starting_room=true (Start a New World... marks its first room).
  2. Choose File → Export All Maps and Tiled World. This saves modified member maps, referenced tilesets and the world, exports every member through the normal Lua/tilemap exporter, and records their placements. A canceled save or failed batch keeps the previous exported generation.
  3. Stop the game in Defold. After installing toolkit changes, restart Defold to clear cached Lua modules, then Project → Reload Editor Scripts.
  4. Run Project → Prepare & Check. On the first import choose Use Tiled World Layout. Preparation imports assets, creates editable room collections, checks connections and elevators, and publishes the layout and minimap together.
  5. Build and choose Play Game with an empty save slot. No Layout Editor export is needed.

World name and room defaults#

Open MVTK.tiled-project with its extensions enabled, then create or open a world. Open World Properties from Tiled’s world controls: the five toolkit fields are added automatically, preserving any existing choices. There is no setup command and no code editing. Save the world after editing; world export also saves it. This applies only inside the toolkit project.

Property Type / meaning
world_name String. Displayed above the full world map. Empty keeps World Map. It does not rename the game or change save identity.
default_biome biomes dropdown. Used when a room's biome is missing, empty, or inherit.
default_music music_tracks dropdown. Synced track ID used when room music is missing, empty, or inherit. Prepare & Check populates this list.
default_ambience ambient_sounds dropdown. Ambient loop independent of music; must have a configured sound component. Room ambient_sound overrides it.
default_map_color Color. Fallback map tint for rooms without biome_color. Leave alpha at zero to use biome/project colors; otherwise choose an opaque tint.

Explicit room selections win. New blank rooms use inherit for their biome; existing rooms keep their choices, including default. To inherit music, clear the room's music value or select inherit. New world defaults start at inherit, preserving existing room behavior. Once you select a track or none for default_music, an explicit room none means silence. World music none supplies silence to inheriting rooms. An absent, empty, or inherit world music default retains the older room none = keep current music behavior. Ambient none means no world/room ambient loop; placed sound objects remain independent. The old room property ambience remains a music alias; use ambient_sound for ambient loops (String or the ambient_sounds custom type).

Music triggers temporarily override the selected track. A trigger with stop_on_exit and no explicit exit track restores the room/world music. Room-to-room ambient changes stop the previous loop, and rooms sharing a loop keep it playing. Sound playback still requires your configured audio assets.

Room biome_color wins over the world tint; otherwise the world tint wins over the biome color. The project's biome-color switch and map opacity still apply. These colors affect the minimap and full map, not the room background.

After changing world properties, run Export All Maps and Tiled World → Prepare & Check → Build. The selected Tiled world's defaults are published with its layout; unrelated maps and other worlds do not inherit them. Unknown default biome, music, or ambient IDs fail preparation with the relevant config path.

Give a region its own music#

A region is an entry in developer/config/biomes.lua. A room joins a region through its biome map property, or through the world's default_biome when its biome is inherit.

  1. Put the region's .ogg or .wav file in developer/assets/music.
  2. Choose Project ▸ Prepare & Check.
  3. Open developer/config/music_tracks_generated.lua and copy the new track's ID.
  4. Open developer/config/biomes.lua.
  5. In the region's entry, add music = "<track ID>".
  6. Save biomes.lua.
  7. Choose Project ▸ Prepare & Check.
  8. Choose Project ▸ Build.
Expected result: Every room in the region that has no music of its own plays the region's track.

A room's own music still wins. A room set to none in a region with music is silent. The world's default_music applies only when the region has no music. ambience = "<ID>" does the same for ambient loops, with an ID from developer/config/ambient_sounds.lua. Leave a region's name as "" to keep it unnamed: the toolkit never shows a region's ID in place of its name. Publishing a Tiled world rejects a region music or ambience ID that is not registered.

Make a large world start faster#

By default the game loads every room's art before play starts. On a big world with regions, it can load only the regions near the player instead.

  1. Stop the game.
  2. Open Project ▸ Game Settings… ▸ Game.
  3. In Large worlds, set Warm rooms to Region.
  4. Set Regions around it to how many regions beyond the current one to load (0-4).
  5. Click Save and Prepare & Check.
  6. Choose Project ▸ Build.
Expected result: The loading screen is shorter. As you walk into a new region, nearby regions load in the background.

The first visit to a room in a region that is not loaded yet may pause briefly. A world whose rooms have no region loads every room, as before. Set Warm rooms to None to load nothing at start. The world budget report from Prepare & Check warns when loading every room at start would exceed the engine's resource limit. The same settings live in developer/config/stream_settings.lua (prewarm_scope, prewarm_adjacent_regions); Game Settings overrides them.

The other Large worlds fields keep a busy world inside the engine's limits:

  • Enemies wait in hidden rooms (on by default): enemies in rooms that are loaded but not on screen stop where they stand and carry on when the room appears. Bosses and elevators always keep running.
  • Live shots (96): the most shots alive at once. Past it the oldest enemy shot disappears; your shots go only when no enemy shot is left. Nothing disappears during a locked boss fight.
  • Live damage numbers (48): extra numbers are skipped. Numbers share game.project's label limit (128) with signs, levers and other labels.

Reward pickups are never capped. The world budget report counts the two caps in its runtime reserve.

Name a region and show its title card#

The first time the player enters a named region, a title card shows its name and subtitle at the top of the screen. The pause menu names the region too.

  1. Open developer/config/biomes.lua.
  2. In the region's entry, set name = "<region name>".
  3. In the same entry, add subtitle = "<one short line>".
  4. Save biomes.lua.
  5. Choose Project ▸ Prepare & Check.
  6. Choose Project ▸ Build.
  7. Play an empty save slot and walk into a room of that region.
Expected result: The card shows the region's name and subtitle once, and the pause menu's info line names the region.

To choose when cards show for every region:

  1. Choose Project ▸ Game Settings….
  2. Open HUD and prompts ▸ Area titles.
  3. Set When to show to first_visit or every_entry.
  4. Set Seconds shown.
  5. Click Save and Prepare & Check.
  6. Choose Project ▸ Build.

HUD and prompts ▸ Show on screen ▸ Area titles turns the cards off. One region can override When to show with title_card = "first_visit", "every_entry" or "never" in its biomes.lua entry. Pause, dialogue, cutscenes and a locked boss arena hold the card until play resumes. An unnamed region (name = "") shows no card and no pause line.

Give a landmark room its own title#

  1. In Tiled, open the room's map.
  2. Choose Map ▸ Map Properties....
  3. Add a string property named room_title and type the landmark's name.
  4. Add a bool property named room_title_card and tick it.
  5. Choose File ▸ Export Current Room.
  6. In Defold, choose Project ▸ Prepare & Check.
  7. Choose Project ▸ Build.
Expected result: The first entry into the room shows a title card with its landmark name, and the pause menu reads "<region name> - <landmark name>".

Leave room_title_card unticked to show the title only in the pause menu. Project ▸ Translations… lists region names, subtitles and room titles.

Re-exporting the same world updates its layout on the next successful preparation. For an edit inside one room, save that map and choose File → Export Current Room, then Prepare & Check → Build in Defold. This exports only the active room's Lua and tilemap and retains the existing world placement. Use the full world export when moving, adding, removing, or resizing rooms, changing the starting room, world properties, or shared tilesets/templates used across the world. Negative positions and gaps remain as authored; touching rooms only connect when their opposing door spans overlap. Overlaps and ambiguous connections are errors. World patterns are resolved by Tiled; onlyShowAdjacentMaps does not limit export. Changed pattern membership or unavailable world sources require another export.

Keep Layout Editor Layout retains manual placement. Project → Use Tiled World Layout can switch later and shows the number of moved, added and removed rooms. Opening Layout Editor while Tiled owns placement shows the published world for inspection. Pan and zoom work; Edit Here explicitly takes a manual copy. Restore Draft resumes the backed-up manual placement instead. After either switch, stop the game and run Prepare & Check → Build before adding rooms that were exported separately from the Tiled world. Then reopen Layout Editor and click Rescan Rooms. World preparation only builds that world's members; manual preparation includes all exported rooms. Rescan refreshes the built room list, so it cannot import or build a newly exported room by itself. The previous manual draft is backed up in developer/runtime/layout/manual_backup.lua. Tags stay with unchanged room IDs; the previous published placement and removed tags are also retained in developer/runtime/layout/previous_world_layout.lua. Source switching never rewrites your .world.

For preparation, use Prepare & Check → Build. Exported world data is under developer/assets/tiled/worlds/; one world owns the runtime layout at a time. If an export is interrupted, run Export All Maps and Tiled World again to recover its journal before preparing. Individual map export remains available.

Use Progression to analyze current placement and preview starting abilities, item counts and flags. Areas, gates and objectives show reachable, blocked or unknown results with explanations. Optional Tiled logical areas prevent internal barriers from being treated as free passage. See Checking progression. Project → Analyze Progression checks the published world. Prepare & Check and export also reject invalid model references; uncertain movement/puzzles remain playtest warnings.

Place the three rooms#

  1. At the title screen, click Layout Editor.
  2. Click Rescan Rooms.
  3. If you want to replace the existing placement, click Clear. This removes layout placements, not your Tiled source files.
  4. Click start_room, middle_room, and goal_room in the Rooms sidebar to add them.
  5. Drag middle_room beside start_room until right ↔ left door markers align and snap.
  6. Drag goal_room beside middle_room until their matching markers snap.

Right connects to left. Top connects to bottom. If rooms will not snap, inspect the painted door_* layers in Tiled, re-export, rebuild, and rescan.

Export the layout#

Adding and moving rooms autosaves Layout Editor working state, but gameplay prefers the published runtime layout. You must explicitly export.

  1. Click Export in Layout Editor.
  2. Wait for the success/status message to finish.
  3. Only then click Menu to return to the title screen.

Layout Export publishes resources including:

  • developer/runtime/layout/world_layout.local.lua
  • minimap PNG/metadata and the map outlines (layout_vectors.lua) under developer/assets/minimap/
  • export/developer/runtime/layout/world_layout.lua
Important: Menu only returns to the title screen. It does not export. Skipping Export can make Play Game load an older layout even though Layout Editor appeared to autosave.

8. Play And Verify The First World#

  1. Click Play Game.
  2. Choose an empty save slot.
  3. On Choose difficulty, select Normal for this first pass.
  4. Confirm the player begins in start_room.
  5. Walk through the opening into middle_room.
  6. Continue into goal_room.
  7. Check collision, placed objects, music changes, and room transitions.

The difficulty picker appears on an empty slot by default. A game configured with fixed difficulty skips it.

Use an empty slot after changing the starting room or world layout. A valid checkpoint in an older save can correctly override the newly authored start.

Expected result: You can traverse all three authored rooms; their art, collision, templates, doors, and room music match Tiled.

9. Common Creator Recipes#

All placed objects go on the visible top-level templates layer unless a recipe says otherwise. Select a template instance to edit its properties in Tiled's Properties panel. Leave mvtk_template_version unchanged.

Understand grouped template properties#

Most templates keep the property that identifies the object, such as enemy_type, plus its required ID at the top level. Less-common settings are organized into one-level collapsible groups such as movement_and_attacks, stats, interaction, or feedback_and_visuals. Expand only the group you need; a collapsed group still contributes its template defaults.

Six tiny placement templates intentionally stay flat because grouping would add more clicks than clarity: solid_box.tx, solid_slope.tx, grapple_box.tx, grapple_slope.tx, one_way_platform.tx, and player_start.tx.

Newly placed templates already include these grouped properties. No upgrade step is needed. Maps made before template version 2.7 can keep their legacy flat overrides; export, validation, Prepare & Check, and runtime accept both layouts. Avoid manually keeping both spellings of the same field on one object.

Reuse a tuned object with a template preset#

Start with one selected toolkit template object on the top-level templates layer of a saved room. A template preset keeps its base type, shape and tuning; placement IDs and object links reset so each placement can have its own links.

  1. Choose File ▸ Save as Template Preset... (also in the object's right-click menu).
  2. Enter a lower-case name such as fast_spikes in Name.
  3. Keep the suggested external presets folder in Folder.
  4. Click OK to save the .tx file.
  5. Drag the preset from the Project view onto the top-level templates layer wherever you need it.
  6. Edit the .tx in Tiled's Template Editor to change every placement that inherits those values.
  7. Choose Save as Template Preset... on a tuned preset placement to open Update preset <name>.
  8. Click OK to update the preset from that placement.
  9. Confirm the overwrite when asked.
  10. Save the affected rooms.
  11. Choose File ▸ Export All Maps and Tiled World.
  12. Choose Project ▸ Prepare & Check in Defold before building.

Presets belong outside the toolkit, beside your original maps and art. If the folder is missing from the Project view, choose Project ▸ Add Folder to Project.... Enemy Art must point to an original skin frame outside the toolkit; the command keeps that link when the preset is saved elsewhere. To convert an existing ordinary placement, place the preset by hand and remove the old object. Per-placement overrides keep their own values when the preset changes. A base-version warning means select a preset placement and update it with this command; there is no Refresh command. All placements bake into editable gameobjects in the room collection.

Replace the player sprite#

The beginner-safe rule is to keep the existing animation IDs and replace art, not controller code.

The optional source-driven importer uses:

  • Template: developer/assets/source/player_template/Mannequin/, a colour-coded figure in every pose the game uses
  • Importer: developer/tools/generate_player_sprite_frames.py
  • Generated frames: developer/assets/sprites/player/
  • Generated atlas: developer/assets/atlas/player.atlas

Install Pillow for the Python interpreter you use. Copy the Mannequin folder beside itself, for example as MyPlayer, replace its sheets with your art (same folders, file names and layout), then run:

py developer/tools/generate_player_sprite_frames.py --source "developer/assets/source/player_template/MyPlayer"

The bundled player art is the Wayfarer pack in developer/assets/source/player_template/Wayfarer/. It is drawn by developer/tools/generate_player_model_pack.py; run py developer/tools/generate_player_model_pack.py to redraw it and import it again. A pack can also add the optional sheets Swim/, Swim Idle/, Ledge Hang/, Grapple/, and Climb Idle (facing back of player)/; when one is missing, the importer uses stand-in frames instead. Ladder climbing always uses the Climb (facing back of player) sheet.

Export frames at their intended in-game pixel size and leave Player Size at 1.00 (100%). The bundled template packs use 192 × 192 frames with crisp 4 × 4 pixel blocks; in Aseprite, export the original 48 × 48 drawings at 400%. The importer copies these frames at 1:1 by default, so one image pixel is one world pixel at Player Size 1.00.

Project ▸ Game Settings… ▸ Player art can import another PNG sheet or a sequence of frames. Leave displayed width and height at 0 to use the frame dimensions.
